Towards univalent reference types
Jonathan Sterling, Daniel Gratzer, Lars Birkedal

Abstract
We develop a denotational semantics for general reference types in an impredicative version of guarded homotopy type theory , an adaptation of synthetic guarded domain theory to Voevodsky’s univalent foundations. We observe for the first time the profound impact of univalence on the denotational semantics of mutable state. Univalence automatically ensures that all computations are invariant under symmetries of the heap—a bountiful source of program equivalences. In particular, even the most simplistic univalent model enjoys many new equations that do not hold when the same constructions are carried out in the universes of traditional set-level (extensional) type theory.

1
Introduction

Moggi
[
32
]
famously distinguished three semantics-based approaches to proving equivalences between programs:
operational
,
denotational
, and
logical
. Operational semantics studies programs
indirectly
by investigating the properties of a transition function that executes programs
qua
code on a highly specific idealized computer; in contrast, denotational semantics views programs
directly
as functions on highly specialized kinds of spaces, without making any detour through transition functions. Moggi’s departure is to advance a
logical
approach to program equivalence, in which a programming language is an equational theory equipped with a
category
of denotational models for which it is both sound and complete.

Moggi’s logical approach to program equivalence therefore subsumes traditional denotational semantics: both the general and the particular necessarily exude their own depth and sophistication, but they are now correctly situated in relation to each other so that workers in semantics can reap the greatest benefits from the
theory–model
dialectic:

1.

Even if there is a distinguished “standard” model of a given programming language (
e.g
.
the Scott model of PCF), any non-trivial investigation of the syntax of that language necessarily involves non-standard models — if only because induction can always be seen as a model construction. These non-standard models include the
generic
model, built from the theory itself, as well as models based on logical relations; thus the need for clear thinking about many models via logical semantics cannot be bypassed.

2.

Conversely, the discovery of a new model of a programming language can inspire and justify the refinement of its equational theory: for instance,
parametric models
have been used to justify equational theories for data abstraction and local store. In the other direction, the discovery of a “non-model” that nonetheless has desirable properties can open up new semantic vistas by motivating a relaxed equational theory.

1.1
State and reference types: static and dynamic allocation

One of the oldest programming constructs is
state
: the ability to read from and write to the computer’s memory as a side effect. Theories of state delineate themselves along two axes: (1) the kinds of data that can be stored, and (2) the kinds of allocations allowed. On the first axis, languages range from being able to store integers and strings (
first-order store
) all the way to being able to store elements of arbitrary types, including closures (
higher-order store
). On the second axis, we have
static allocation
on one end, where the type of a function specifies exactly what kind of state it uses, and
dynamic allocation
on the other end, where the types and quantity of memory cells allocated are revealed only during execution. Under dynamic allocation, one has
reference types
whose elements are
pointers
to memory cells storing elements of a given type.

1.2
Equational theories of dynamic storage: between local and global

The semantics of state are only difficult under dynamic allocation; indeed, computations that interact with a statically known heap configuration
\overline{\ell_{i}:\sigma_{i}}
can be classified by Moggi’s state monad
\sigma\to\sigma\times-
where
\sigma:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{i}}{\textstyle\prod_{i}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}
, and it is reasonable to
define
the equational theory of static allocation by means of this interpretation. The equational theory of
dynamic
storage is by contrast far from solidified: the introduction of dynamic allocation opens up a spectrum of abstraction between what may be called
local store
and
global store
.

Global store is the least abstract theory of dynamic allocation: in a model of global store, it is permitted that allocations be globally observable regardless of their impact on the results of computations. For instance, global store models are allowed to distinguish the program
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,\texttt{``hello''};\mathsf{ret}\,10$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,\texttt{``hello''};\mathsf{ret}\,10$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,\texttt{``hello''};\mathsf{ret}\,10$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,\texttt{``hello''};\mathsf{ret}\,10$}\mathclose{}{)}}
from the simpler program
\mathsf{ret}\,10
. By contrast, models of
local store
validate equations resembling an idealized garbage collector, in which the heap is only observable through its abstract read/write interface; in a model of local store, we necessarily have
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,\texttt{``hello''};\mathsf{ret}\,10$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,\texttt{``hello''};\mathsf{ret}\,10$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,\texttt{``hello''};\mathsf{ret}\,10$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,\texttt{``hello''};\mathsf{ret}\,10$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{ret}\,10
as well as many other equations.

The abstraction offered by local store is highly desirable. Moreover, Staton
[
42
]
has shown that Plotkin and Power’s algebraic theory of
first-order
local store
[
38
]
is complete in the extremely strong sense that it derives any consistent equation. Beyond first-order references, the very definition of the local store theory becomes less clear, and so a landscape of intermediate theories has emerged in the search for well-behaved models. For example, Kammar
et al
.

[
27
]
have constructed a compelling model of local
full ground store
, going beyond first-order store by allowing pointers to pointers. On the other hand, Levy
[
28
,
29
,
30
]
has given a domain theoretic model of the
global
allocation theory of higher-order store.

1.3
Semantic worlds and guarded models of higher-order store

Denotational models of full dynamic allocation, such as those of Plotkin and Power
[
38
]
, Levy
[
28
]
, and Kammar
et al
.

[
27
]
, tend to share an important limitation: in the model, a semantic program can only allocate a memory cell with a
syntactic
type. This restriction is quite unnatural and impractical in the context of higher-order store, where many important program equivalences actually follow from the presence of exotic semantic types lying outside the image of the interpretation function (
e.g
.
in relational models à la Girard and Reynolds).

The search for models of general references closed under allocation of cells with
semantic
types has been major motivation of current work in
guarded domain theory
, expressed in operational semantics by
step-indexing

[
8
,
3
]
and in denotational semantics by means of various generalizations of metric space
[
10
,
6
,
21
,
17
]
.
The problem solved by guarded domain theory is the following famous circularity described in several prior works
[
3
,
9
,
17
]
:

1.

A semantic type needs to be some kind of covariant family of predomains indexed in the possible configurations of the heap (“worlds”); a single predomain won’t do, because the elements of type
\mathsf{IORef}\,{\sigma}
vary depending on what cells have been allocated.

2.

A semantic world should be a finite mapping from memory locations to semantic types.

Guarded domain theory approximates a solution to the domain equation evoked above by decreasing precision at every recursive occurrence. Although it may be possible to find a fully precise solution to this domain equation using traditional domain theory, Birkedal
et al
.

[
18
, §5]
have presented evidence that such a fixed point will
not
brook the interpretation of reference types by a continuous function on the domain of all types, ruling out semantics for recursive types. Thus guarded domain theory or step-indexing would seem to be mandatory for
functional
models of general reference types with semantic worlds.
1
1

1

A non-functional approach to compositionality for reference types would be the expression of Reynolds’
capability interpretation
of references
[
39
]
in game semantics by Abramsky, Honda, and McCusker
[
2
]
.

Models of guarded domain theory can be embedded into topoi whose internal language is referred to as
synthetic guarded domain theory
or
SGDT
; the most famous of these topoi is the
topos of trees

[
17
]
given by presheaves on
\omega
.
The idea of using synthetic guarded domain theory as a setting for the naïve denotational semantics of programming languages with general recursion was first explored by Paviotti, Møgelberg, and Birkedal
[
36
,
31
,
35
]
.

Sterling, Gratzer, and Birkedal
[
43
]
have recently extended the program of Paviotti
et al
.
to the general case of full higher-order store with polymorphism and recursive types: in particular,
op. cit
.
have shown how to model general reference types in synthetic guarded domain theory assuming an impredicative universe (as can be found in realizability models
[
25
,
26
]
). This model is the starting point of the present paper: by adapting the construction of Sterling
et al
.
to the setting of univalent foundations, we obtain a new suite of equational reasoning principles that we refer to as the
theory of univalent reference types
.

1.4
Univalent reference types and data abstraction in the heap

The thesis of this paper is that Voevodsky’s
univalence
principle leads to simpler models of general reference types that nonetheless validate extraordinarily strong equations between stateful programs. To examine this claim, we consider the type of object-oriented counters in a Haskell-like language:

\mathsf{Counter}:\equiv\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{incr}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}};\mathsf{read}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Int}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{incr}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}};\mathsf{read}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Int}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{incr}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}};\mathsf{read}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Int}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{incr}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}};\mathsf{read}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Int}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}

The most obvious implementation of the
\mathsf{Counter}
interface simply allocates an integer and increments it in memory as follows:

\mathsf{posCounter}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Counter}

\mathsf{posCounter}:\equiv\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,0;\mathsf{ret}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i+1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{\}}}

Another implementation might count
backwards
and then negate the stored value on
\mathsf{read}
using the functorial action
\mathsf{map}
of
\mathsf{IO}
on
\mathsf{neg}:\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Int}
:

\mathsf{negCounter}:\equiv\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,0;\mathsf{ret}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{map}\,\mathsf{neg}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{map}\,\mathsf{neg}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{map}\,\mathsf{neg}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{map}\,\mathsf{neg}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}

By intuition, the
\mathsf{posCounter}
and
\mathsf{negCounter}
implementations of the counter interface should be “observationally equivalent” in the sense that no context of ground type should be able to distinguish them: indeed, even though
\mathsf{negCounter}
is writing negative numbers to the heap instead of positive numbers, the only way a context can observe the allocated cell is using the
\mathsf{read}
method. The observational equivalence
\mathsf{posCounter}\simeq\mathsf{negCounter}
is typically proved using a
relational model
, as both Birkedal
et al
.

[
18
, §6.3]
and Sterling
et al
.

[
43
]
did.

Observational equivalence is not the same as equality, neither in syntax nor semantics. Indeed, typical equational theories of (local or global) dynamic allocation do
not
derive the equation
\vdash\mathsf{posCounter}\equiv\mathsf{negCounter}
, as can be seen easily by means of a countermodel: we have
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{posCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{posCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{posCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{posCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}\not=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{negCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{negCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{negCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{negCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
in both the relational models of
op. cit
.
, although it is true that
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{posCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{posCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{posCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{posCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}\mathrel{R_{\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Counter}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{negCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{negCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{negCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{negCounter}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
holds. The observational equivalence
\mathsf{posCounter}\simeq\mathsf{negCounter}
is deduced in the relational models because the relation on
observations
is discrete.

What distinguishes our
univalent reference types
from ordinary reference types is that the former actually derive equations like
\vdash\mathsf{posCounter}\equiv\mathsf{negCounter}
, as shown in
\cref
thm:counter. We substantiate this equational theory by constructing a model (
\cref
thm:the-model) in univalent foundations
[
46
]
in which the equation
\vdash\mathsf{posCounter}\equiv\mathsf{negCounter}
follows immediately from the univalence principle of the metalanguage. Although it may be possible to validate this equation using non-standard parametric models (or less scrupulously by an extensional collapse), our contribution is to show that it also holds in a “standard” model, provided that this standard model is constructed in a univalent metatheory.

2
A higher-order language with (univalent) reference types

We begin by giving a description of the syntax and the equational theory of a simple language with
references. The language is meant to be
as simple as possible, but no simpler
. In
particular, it contains several problematic constructs (higher-order store, dynamic allocations,

etc
.
) that have been historically difficult to model in denotational semantics.

types
::=

τ
,
σ

σ
→
τ
∣
IO

τ
∣
IORef

τ
∣…

terms
::=

e,e’

x
∣
rec
f x
in
e
∣
ret
e ∣x ←e; e’
∣
alloc
e ∣
get
e ∣
set
e e’
∣
step

∣…

\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{alloc}\,e:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{IORef}\,\sigma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{IORef}\,\sigma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{IORef}\,\sigma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{IORef}\,\sigma$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\mathsf{IORef}\,\sigma}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{get}\,e:\mathsf{IO}\,\sigma}\frac{\Gamma\vdash e:\mathsf{IORef}\,\sigma\quad\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:\sigma}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{set}\,e\,e^{\prime}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{step}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\frac{\Gamma,f:\sigma\to\mathsf{IO}\,\tau,x:\sigma\vdash e:\mathsf{IO}\,\tau}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{rec}~f\,x~\mathsf{in}~e:\sigma\to\mathsf{IO}\,\tau}\frac{\Gamma,f:\sigma\to\mathsf{IO}\,\tau,x:\sigma\vdash e:\mathsf{IO}\,\tau\quad\Gamma\vdash \displaystyle e:\mathsf{IORef}\,\sigma,e^{\prime}:\sigma

to be the “abstract interface” of a reference cell. Here we write
\mathsf{map}\,f:\mathsf{IO}\,A\to\mathsf{IO}\,B
for the functorial action of
\mathsf{IO}
on a function
f:A\to B
.

2.1
The equational theory of monadic general reference types

Although there are many different ways to present programming languages with side effects, for the sake of familiarity we have chosen to focus on
a variant of Moggi’s
monadic metalanguage

[
32
]
.
2
2

2

When developing our denotational semantics in
\cref
sec:semantics-in-uf, we will refine the monadic point of view by passing to an adjoint call-by-push-value resolution of the computational monad
[
29
]
.
Essentially, this is a
simply-typed lambda calculus supplemented with a
strong monad

\mathsf{IO}
and further equipped
with a type of references
\mathsf{IORef}\,\tau
along with a suite of effectful operations for interacting with references. Like in Haskell, all side effects are confined to the monad; unlike Haskell, general recursion is treated as a side effect.

One non-standard aspect of our language bears special attention, namely the nullary side effect
\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{step}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. This effect can be thought of as the “exhaust” left behind in the equational theory by unfolding any kind of recursively defined construct, including not only the unfolding of recursive functions but also accesses to the heap. In particular, for a given recursive function
g:\equiv\mathsf{rec}~f\,x~\mathsf{in}~e
, we do not have
\vdash g\,e^{\prime}\equiv\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle g/f,e^{\prime}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle g/f,e^{\prime}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle g/f,e^{\prime}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle g/f,e^{\prime}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}e
but rather only
\vdash g\,e^{\prime}\equiv\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{step};\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle g/f,e^{\prime}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle g/f,e^{\prime}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle g/f,e^{\prime}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle g/f,e^{\prime}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}e
. Likewise, our equational theory does not equate
\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,e;\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,e;\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,e;\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,e;\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv\mathsf{ret}\,e
but rather only
\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,e;\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,e;\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,e;\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,e;\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{step};\mathsf{ret}\,e
. The presence of
\mathsf{step}
in our equational theory is forced by the
guarded
denotational semantics that we will later employ in
\cref
sec:semantics-in-uf,thm:the-model.

2.2
The equational theory of univalent reference types

The equational theory of
univalent reference types
strengthens
\cref
fig:rules by quotienting under symmetries of the heap, expressed in the two rules depicted in
\cref
fig:univalent.

1.

The
allocation permutation
rule states that the order in which references are allocated does not matter; this is a kind of
nominal symmetry
built into the theory of univalent reference types, expressing that the
layout
of the heap is viewed up to isomorphism.

2.

The
representation independence
rule states that the
observable interface
of a reference cell is invariant under isomorphisms of that cell’s contents.

The
allocation permutation
rule is common to theories of local dynamic allocation, but less common in theories of global dynamic allocation. The
representation independence
rule is, however, a new feature of univalent reference types that goes beyond existing local theories of dynamic allocation: as we have discussed in
\cref
sec:intro:univalent-reference-types, such a law typically holds up to observational equivalence but almost never “on the nose” at higher type. It is therefore worth going into more detail.

The idea of
representation independence
is that allocating a cell of type
\sigma
and then only interacting with it by means of its
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{get},\mathsf{set}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{get},\mathsf{set}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{get},\mathsf{set}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{get},\mathsf{set}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
methods should be the same as allocating a cell of a different type
\tau
and interacting with it by conjugating its
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{get},\mathsf{set}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{get},\mathsf{set}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{get},\mathsf{set}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{get},\mathsf{set}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
interface by an isomorphism
e:\sigma\cong\tau
. In particular, it is allowed that
\sigma\equiv\tau
and
e:\sigma\cong\sigma
be nonetheless a non-trivial automorphism: and so we may derive from
representation independence
our case study involving imperative counters that count forward and backwards.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\mathsf{Counter}
,
\mathsf{posCounter}
, and
\mathsf{negCounter}
be as in
\cref
sec:intro:univalent-reference-types:

\mathsf{Counter}:\equiv\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{incr}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}};\mathsf{read}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Int}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{incr}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}};\mathsf{read}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Int}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{incr}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}};\mathsf{read}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Int}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{incr}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{)}};\mathsf{read}:\mathsf{IO}\,\mathsf{Int}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}

\mathsf{negCounter}:\equiv\ell\leftarrow\mathsf{alloc}\,0;\mathsf{ret}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathsf{read}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{map}\,\mathsf{neg}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{get}\,\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{incr}\hookrightarrow i\leftarrow\mathsf{get}\,\ell;\mathsf{set}\,\ell\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i-1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle \mathsf{neg}:\mathsf{Int}\to\mathsf{Int}
sending an integer to its negation is a self-dual automorphism; we therefore calculate from left to right.

3
Denotational semantics in univalent foundations

We now turn to the construction of a model of univalent reference types. At the coarsest level,
this model follows the standard template for a model with mutable state: types are interpreted by
covariant presheaves on a certain category of
worlds
with each world describing the
collection of references available and the (semantic) type associated to each. The type of
references
\mathsf{IORef}\,\tau
assigns each world to the collection of locations of appropriate type
while the monad
\mathsf{IO}
is then interpreted by a certain
store-passing
monad.

This simple picture is quickly complicated by the need to model general store: semantic types must
reference worlds which in turn reference semantic types. This naturally leads us to synthetic
guarded domain theory (SGDT) in order to cope with the circularity. This alone, however, is
insufficient. While SGDT allows us to define the category of worlds, the resulting solution is a

large type
—at least the size of the universe of semantic types. This becomes a problem when it comes time to model the state monad, which must quantify over all possible worlds for its input and return a new world for its output. To model these large products and sums of worlds, we will base our model on an
impredicative
universe: impredicativity implies that the category of covariant presheaves on our large category of worlds is (locally) cartesian closed and supports all the structure of our language.

We present some of the prerequisites for our model in

\cref
sec:semantics:preliminaries. In
\cref
sec:semantics:model we
construct the model of univalent reference types.

3.1
Univalent impredicative synthetic guarded domain theory

We work informally in the language of homotopy type theory
[
40
,
46
]
; in this section, we briefly describe some of our preferred conventions.
When we speak of “existence”, we shall always mean
mere
existence. Categories are always assumed to be univalent 1-categories; given a category
\mathscr{X}
, we will write
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{X}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{X}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{X}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{X}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
for its underlying 1-type of objects.
Rather than fixing a global hierarchy of universes, we assume universes locally where needed. In this paper, all universes are assumed to be univalent; when we wish to assume that a universe is closed under the connectives of Martin-Löf type theory (dependent products, dependent sums, finite coproducts, W-types,
etc
.
) we will refer to it as a
Martin-Löf universe
. We will not belabor the difference between codes and types.

3.1.1
Impredicative subuniverses in univalent foundations

Recall that a type
A
is called
\mathcal{U}
-small
if and only if there exists a (necessarily unique) code
\hat{A}:\mathcal{U}
together with an equivalence
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hat{A}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\hat{A}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hat{A}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hat{A}$}\mathclose{}{]}}\simeq A
, and a family is
\mathcal{U}
-small when each of its fibers are.
A
reflection
of
A
in a universe
\mathcal{U}
is, by contrast, defined to be a (necessarily unique) function
\eta:A\to A_{\mathcal{U}}
with
A_{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathcal{U}
such for any type
C\in\mathcal{U}
, the precomposition map
C^{\eta}:C^{A_{\mathcal{U}}}\to C^{A}
is an equivalence. When a reflection of
A
in
\mathcal{U}
exists (necessarily uniquely), we shall say that
A
is reflected in
\mathcal{U}
.

A
subuniverse
of a universe
\mathcal{U}
is defined to be a dependent type
A:\mathcal{U}\vdash PA~\mathit{type}
such that each
PA
is a proposition. We may write
\mathcal{U}_{P}
for the universe
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{PA}
obtained by restricting
\mathcal{U}
to the elements satisfying
P
. We will frequently abuse notation implicitly identifying the predicate coding a subuniverse with its comprehension as an actual type.
A subuniverse
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is said to be
reflective
if every
A:\mathcal{U}
is reflected in
\mathcal{S}
. A subuniverse of
\mathcal{U}
is said to be “small” when its comprehension as a type is
\mathcal{U}
-small.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a universe closed under dependent products. A subuniverse
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is said to be a
dependent exponential ideal
if for every
A:\mathcal{U}
and
B:A\to\mathcal{S}
, the dependent product
\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{x:A}}{\textstyle\prod_{x:A}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{x:A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{x:A}}{Bx}
lies in
\mathcal{S}
. An
impredicative subuniverse
of
\mathcal{U}
is defined to be a small, dependent exponential ideal
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. It is proved by Rijke, Shulman, and Spitters
[
41
]
that any reflective subuniverse of
\mathcal{U}
is a dependent exponential ideal of
\mathcal{U}
.
We will refer to a subuniverse
\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
is impredicative in
\mathcal{U}
as
set-impredicative
; we will refer to
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
as
set-reflective
when
\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
is reflective in
\mathcal{U}
. Under suitable assumptions, these two conditions are in fact equivalent:

Theorem 2
.

A small
\Sigma
-closed subuniverse
\mathcal{S}
of a Martin-Löf universe
\mathcal{U}
is set-impredicative and closed under identity types if and only if it is set-reflective.

Proof.

This can be shown using the methods of Awodey, Frey, and Speight
[
13
]
.
∎

By virtue of
\cref
thm:set-level-impred-to-reflective, we see that small reflective subuniverses are just another presentation of the impredicative universes that appear in the Calculus of Constructions.

3.1.2
The Hofmann–Streicher universe

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a small subuniverse of a Martin-Löf universe
\mathcal{U}
, and let
\mathscr{X}
be a
\mathcal{U}
-small category; we can define the
Hofmann–Streicher lifting

[
24
,
12
]
of
\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
as co-presheaf of 1-types on
\mathscr{X}
. Formally, this means constructing a functor from the 1-category
\mathscr{X}
to the (2,1)-category
1\mathsf{Type}_{\mathcal{U}}
of 1-types in
\mathcal{U}
; thus we depend technically on the account of bicategories in univalent foundations due to Ahrens
et al
.

[
4
]
.
3
3

3

We differ from the conventions of Ahrens
et al
.

[
4
]
: we will say “2-category” to mean
univalent bicategory
in the sense of
op. cit
.
, as we are not at all concerned with the strict notions considered there. Therefore, a “(2,1)-category” in our sense refers to a 2-category whose 2-cells are given by identifications.

Remark 3
.

The purpose of introducing the Hofmann–Streicher lifting in such detail is give some structure to the otherwise bewildering
\cref
con:presheaf-to-hs-code, which plays a crucial technical role in the definition of univalent reference types.

Construction 4

(The Hofmann–Streicher lifting)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a small subuniverse of a Martin-Löf universe
\mathcal{U}
, and let
\mathscr{X}
be a
\mathcal{U}
-small category.
We may define a 2-functor
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}:\mathscr{X}\to 1\mathsf{Type}_{\mathcal{U}}
called the
Hofmann–Streicher lifting
of
\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
as follows:

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}U:\equiv\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle \mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f:U\to V$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f:U\to V$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f:U\to V$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f:U\to V$}\mathclose{}{)}}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle E:U/\mathscr{X}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle E:U/\mathscr{X}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle E:U/\mathscr{X}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle E:U/\mathscr{X}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\equiv E\circ\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f/\mathscr{X}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f/\mathscr{X}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f/\mathscr{X}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f/\mathscr{X}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

When
\mathscr{X}
is viewed as a 2-category, the 2-cells are given by identifications. Thus the 2-functoriality of
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}
and all related coherences are defined by path induction.

Construction 5

(Restricting co-presheaves)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a small subuniverse of a Martin-Löf universe
\mathcal{U}
, and let
\mathscr{X}
be a
\mathcal{U}
-small category. Every co-presheaf
E:\mathscr{X}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
determines a global element
\mathbf{1}\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\displaystyle E$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\textstyle E$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}_{U}:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}U
natural in
U\in\mathscr{X}
by setting
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\displaystyle E$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\textstyle E$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}_{U}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f:U\to V$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f:U\to V$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f:U\to V$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f:U\to V$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\equiv EV
.

3.1.3
(Higher) synthetic guarded domain theory

We adapt Birkedal
et al
.
’s formulation
[
19
]
of dependently typed guarded recursion to the setting of homotopy type theory. In particular, we introduce a new syntactic sort of
delayed substitutions

\vdash\xi\leadsto\Xi
simultaneously with a new type former
\vdash\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\xi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\xi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\xi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}\mathclose{}{]}}.A~\mathit{type}
called the
later modality
,
4
4

4

The “later modality” is
not
a modality in the sense of Rijke, Shulman, and Spitters
[
41
]
, but rather in the older and more general sense of modality in type theory or logic.
whose introduction form is written
\mathsf{next}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\xi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\xi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\xi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\xi$}\mathclose{}{]}}.a
; we summarize the rules for the later modality in
\cref
fig:later.
The
raison d’être
for the later modality is to form
guarded fixed points
: in particular, if we have
f:\mathord{\blacktriangleright}A\to A
, there is a
unique
element
\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}:A
such that
f\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{next}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{next}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{next}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{next}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}{f}
. In particular, this gives unique fixed points for any function
f:A\to A
factoring on the left through
\mathsf{next}:A\to\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{A}
.

, making
\mathord{\blacktriangleright}
left exact. We will write
\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{A}
and
\mathsf{next}\,{a}
for
\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{]}}.A
and
\mathsf{next}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{]}}.a
respectively. For the remaining rules, we refer the reader to the description of Bizjak and Møgelberg
[
20
]
.

Definition 6
.

A
guarded (
n
-)domain
is an (
n
-)type
A
equipped with the structure of a
\mathord{\blacktriangleright}
-algebra,
i.e
.
a function
\vartheta_{A}:\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{A}\to A
.

We will refer to a (sub)universe closed under later modalities as a
guarded (sub)universe
. For any universe
\mathcal{S}
, we may consider the category
\mathsf{0Dom}_{\mathcal{S}}
of
guarded 0-domains
in
\mathcal{S}
,
i.e
.
sets
A:\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
equipped with a mapping
\vartheta_{A}:\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{A}\to A
.

Lemma 7
.

If
\mathcal{S}
is a guarded universe closed under binary coproducts, then the forgetful functor
\mathsf{R}:\mathsf{0Dom}_{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
has a left adjoint
\mathsf{L}:\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathsf{0Dom}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

We define
\mathsf{L}{A}
by solving the domain equation
\mathsf{L}A\cong A+\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{\mathsf{L}{A}}
via the following guarded fixed point construction in
\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
, using both guarded structure and binary coproducts:

\mathsf{L}A:\equiv\mathsf{fix}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda X:\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}}.A+\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}.Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\lambda X:\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}}.A+\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}.Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lambda X:\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}}.A+\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}.Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda X:\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}}.A+\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Y\leftarrow X$}\mathclose{}{]}}.Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\qed

Notation 8
.

We will write
\mathsf{now}:A\to\mathsf{R}{\mathsf{L}{A}}
for the unit of the adjunction
\mathsf{L}\dashv\mathsf{R}
.

Lemma 9

(Later modality in presheaves)
.

Given a guarded set-reflective small subuniverse
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
and a
\mathcal{U}
-small category

\mathscr{X}
, the later modality from
\mathcal{S}
lifts (with all its operations) into

\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
pointwise,
i.e
.
for any
A\in\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
we may define

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathord{\blacktriangleright}{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}U:\equiv\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle AU$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle AU$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle AU$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle AU$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

3.2
Models of univalent general reference types

To construct our model of higher-order store (
\cref
sec:the-model), we must construct a suitable category of recursively defined semantic worlds (
\cref
sec:semantics:worlds) whose co-presheaves admit reference types (
\cref
con:ref-copresheaf) and a strong monad for higher-order store (
\cref
sec:cbpv-adjunction).

3.2.1
Worlds as univalent heap configurations

Let
\mathsf{Inj}
be the category of finite sets and embeddings; by univalence, any two equipollent finite sets are identified. We now
define the basic elements of worlds
qua
heap configurations.

Definition 10

(The displayed category of families)
.

For any 1-type
X
, we define the displayed category
[
5
]

\mathsf{IFam}_{X}
of
\mathsf{Inj}
-families in
X
over
\mathsf{Inj}
as follows:

1.

over a finite set
I:\mathsf{Inj}
, a displayed object of
\mathsf{IFam}_{X}
is a function
\partial_{I}:I\to X
;

2.

over a function
f:I\to J
between finite sets, a displayed morphism from
\partial_{I}
to
\partial_{J}
is a path
\partial_{f}:\partial_{J}\circ f=\partial_{I}
in
I\to X
.

Definition 11

(The category of bags)
.

For any 1-type
X
, the category of
\mathsf{Inj}
-bags in
X
is defined to be the total category
\mathsf{IBag}_{X}:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\int_{\mathsf{Inj}}}{\textstyle\int_{\mathsf{Inj}}}{\scriptstyle\int_{\mathsf{Inj}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\int_{\mathsf{Inj}}}\mathsf{IFam}_{X}
of the displayed category of finite families in
X
. We will write
U\equiv\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}},\partial_{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}},\partial_{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}},\partial_{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}},\partial_{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for an object of
\mathsf{IBag}_{X}
.

Definition 12
.

For a universe
\mathcal{S}
, we define the category
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}}
of
worlds
simultaneously with its category of
\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
-valued co-presheaves on
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}}
to be the unique solution to the guarded recursive domain equation

\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathsf{IBag}_{\mathord{\blacktriangleright}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\ .

Construction.

The system of equations above is solved internally
[
16
]
by Löb induction in any guarded Martin-Löf universe
\mathcal{S}^{+}
containing
\mathcal{S}
.

Of course,
E
is the 1-type of objects of the functor category
\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
∎

We shall require the following technical observation:

Lemma 13

(Structure identity principle for presheaves)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a universe and let
\mathscr{X}
be a category; for any
A,B:\mathsf{Fun}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{X},\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, let
A\cong B
be the type of natural isomorphisms between presheaves. Then the canonical map
A=B\to A\cong B
is an equivalence.

We now come to the construction of the univalent reference type constructor.

Construction 14

(Univalent references)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a small, guarded,
\Sigma
-closed set-reflective subuniverse of a guarded Martin-Löf universe

on

f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ell$}\mathclose{}{)}},\psi\leftarrow\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}.\,\mathsf{ap}_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}{\mathopen{}{\lfloor}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rfloor}}f}\psi$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Proof.

That the identification
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\displaystyle X$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\textstyle X$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptstyle X$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle X$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}_{U}=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}_{U}
is
\mathcal{S}
-small follows from
\cref
lem:sip:presheaves, using the fact that
U/\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}}
is
\mathcal{U}
-small because
\mathcal{S}
is assumed
\mathcal{U}
-small.
∎

3.2.2
A strong monad for general store

Rather than constructing the monad for general store all at once by hand, we take a more bite-sized approach by decomposing it into a simpler call-by-push-value adjunction following Levy
[
29
]
. In fact, we go
quite a bit further than this and decompose the call-by-push-value adjunction itself into three
separate and simpler adjunctions; the advantage of our decomposition is that it reveals the simple and elegant source of the admittedly complex explicit constructions of
op. cit
. All these adjunctions will be suitably
enriched
so as to give rise to a strong monad.
5
5

5

The purpose of strength, as ever, to transform the global Kleisli extension operation of the monad into a
binding
-operation that applies in arbitrary contexts.

To get started, we will first require the concept of a
heaplet
, which is the valuation of a heap configuration at a particular world, assigning each
specified location to an element of the prescribed semantic type at that world.

Construction 15

(The heaplet distributor)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be guarded universe closed under finite products. We may define a distributor
\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{S}}:\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}
like so:

\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{S}}:\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\mathcal{S}}

\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle U,V$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle U,V$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ell:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ell:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ell:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle U$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle
